Longshore Drive, Ann Arbor, MI(~09/69 — 06/70 )

Resuming school at Ann Arbor got off to a mixed start. The one I thought I wanted to work with, Leslie. Kish , was on sabbatical in Europe. Curiously. His office was occupied by the social psychologist I’d known in ‘64, Daniel Katz . That should’ve been my golden opportunity to pursue a degree in social psychology. I didn’t respond. I didn’t know what I was looking for, but I did not think Daniel Katz was it.

My student assistantship was effectively with Irene Hess, who ran the Sampling Section of the Institute for Social Research in Kish’s absence. I took social psychology review courses but as a student I was somewhere between diffident and indifferent.


writes Tom Mitchell , August 20, 1969 about Security services monitoring mail, Army catch 22, benefits of the military, Capt. Dale Noyd.

531 Long shore. Drive, Apt B
Ann Arbor, MI 48105


Dear Tom,
Just the receipt of your letter was a delight; reading it was even more so. For the first time in a long time, look forward to sitting down and communicating with someone by letter. The results may well he dismal, but bear with me.

Not having received a speedy reply from you, i.e. within a week, I inferred that you had changed your address; and perhaps moved to another part of the country, the possibility of communication forever lost. ( it being somewhat difficult for me to conceive of anyone maintaining the same address for longer than two years) To resolve my doubt I had even considered sending you a pre- addressed post card just to ascertain whether you had indeed received my letter or whether the post office had assigned it to a dead letter file without bothering to return it. Thus my delight at merely receiving a letter from you.

To give some form to this missive I shall begin by answering or answering at your questions. Do I seriously suspect that your mail would he monitored? Not unless you were simultaneously the president of a viable SDS Chanter, chairman of the Huey Long Defamation League, Founder of Friends for Fidel, progenitor of Plaquemine Parrish for Blacks, and author of "Miscegenation Now". It probably cost the FBI more than a dozen men full time to bug King and evaluate the results. How much would it cost them to sample every liberal student. So I think there is really no point in your sealing your letters with scotch tape.

As regards the Army, my feelings are mixed. The Army has a considerable capacity for good as will as for bad. There is a measure of Heller's Catch—22. For example take two trainees Who exhibit identical symptoms of unfitness or unsuitability, (these are in practice well, albeit illogically defined categories of behavior for which a man may be discharged under conditions other than dishonorable) It may be persistent bed wetting, persistent erection coupled with an undue fond— ness for group showering or an uncontrollable inclination toward violence. If one of the men demonstrates genuine desire to remain in the Army, he will be expeditiously processed out. If the other man lets it be known that he dislikes the Army and wants out, his processing will at best be delayed and he may well spend his full two years in the Army. The typical First Sergeant reasoning is obvious: the first man who wants to stay in isn't faking, I don't want a dud like that to stay in the Army, the second man is obviously faking, make the coward suffer. A corollary of Catch 22. On the good side, the Army offers a taste of equality for Negros, a taste which will add to the collective demand for equality in civilian life. Of course one can argue that this effect is not due to, but in spite of the Army, an argument which has some merit, but which is an oversimplification on a number of planes.

The Army is incredibly paternalistic; the paternalistic elements are becoming less a part of the Army-as-a-society and more a part of Army as an institution. i.e. more a socialistic organization and less a fraternal one. That factor coupled with the draft can have a beneficial effect on national life and politics. Draftees are given very good lessons in authoritarianism, seeing how objectionable and arbitrary it can be. At the same time one can see some of the advantages of guaranteed medical care. If you think that I am trying to be facetious, let me state definitely that am not. I entered with men who had never visited a dentist in their lives. In my opinion they got reasonably good treatment, very possibly better treatment than they will get subsequently. The Navy you may recall openly advertises for men with correctable debilities, more or less promising considerable corrective treatment in exchange for enlistment. I'm sure many Europeans are shocked by the implications about American medical treatment which are inherent in those advertisements. It is really a ghastly admissions that much needed medical treatment is denied Americans for lack of money.

There were many men, for whom I had no mean respect, for whom the Army was simply the best opportunity in terms of self—realization and in terms of money. There were many southern and northern WASP's who had to swallow their bigotry when they were assigned to colored commanders. And there were many men who were just simply deserving of respect, for their integrity and humanity.

On the other hand, men given to casual brutality are given positions of responsibility. There is an unmistakable redneck cast to the ranks of NCO's perhaps therein lies much of the origin of the pointless brutality. But I think there is brutality in all of us. I knew and in some ways Liked a fellow officer who proudly told me of one his clever devices. But first I must give some background. The role of the brigade to which I was assigned was to turn out basic trainees. This involved weeks of training followed by tests on each man for information, skills, and physical condition. Accepted and somewhat dishonest practice and repetition of the test material usually suffice for the information and skills parts, but whipping some men into physical shape in the time allotted was a little more difficult. Each man that didn't pass was a mark against the company commander, hut if a man was not medically fit to take the physical test, he could be passed with no mark against the C O. Enter Lt D 's device. With observation he could select those men who were likely failures. Solution: give those men "remedial training" of crawling over rocks in hope that they would be injured just enough to be excused by medical personnel from the physical test.

As far as an underground within the Army, I know no more of it than what I read in national publications. I was never assigned to the unit in which underground activity was reasonably possible. Trainees are for the most part too disoriented, too isolated, too occupied to consider collective resistance. One of the military's biggest worries seemed to be pot. Apparently large numbers acquired a taste for pot in Viet Nam. It seems ironic that one effect of the war may he increased pressure for legalizing pot. Already some states are lowering penalties for possession ( insure convictions so the legislators say, but I'm sure that more are seeing the possibility of their children being arrested)

Enjoyed your piece on the astronauts, luckily I missed most Of the TV inanities— we don't own one, we went to the Illini Union and gawked with all the other non—owners of TV's (strange people all). One thing I can't agree with is your equating Nixon with a sideshow barker. I can define my feeling about a sideshow barker; I can't about Noxin. I am aware of a feeling of disappointment and a feeling that the American people betrayed themself Nov 22-No that's the day Kennedy was assassinated, wasn't it?— But about Noxiou there is the personal feeling of impotence I felt last fall as I watched his inevitable slide to the presidency not unlike helplessly watching evil prevail, but no he cannot be simply equated with evil despite his unholy alliance with Strom Thurmond, which alliance did neither one, of then Credit. Hearing his platitude one was tempted to say insipid but how can something insipid so decidedly have a taste albeit undefinable? The white house has not been turned into that three ring glamor circus that obscured an objective evaluation of Kennedv. Neither have we yet suffered through Johnsonian flamboyance and petulance nor Eisenhower inaction. The manipulaion of the Warren resignation seemed tricky Dickerv at its worst yet surprise of surprises, Noxious seems bent on keening some campaign promises — supreme trickery? The only terms on which Nixon seems to he comprehensible to me is that he is trying to be all not—things to all men. Nix—on-everything By negating so much, it is inevitable that he do some good. The poor affirmator, HHH, even affirmed the war.

When Nixon was nominated, I thought that the Democrats had it given to them on a platter. All they would have to do would be to run excerpts from Nixon's tearful farewell to politics without comment on TV. I thought thenomination to be the final spasm of the GOP, rewarding loyalty without any due consideration to reason or even expediency. How wrong can one be. Not having a TV, I don't know, did the Demo's run excerpts of Nixon's good— bye to politics?

Jumping around a bit with respect to the order of information in your letter, no I haven't read the New Legions, but if you recommend it in the same breath with Catch 22, should like to. Re: inevitable change within the Army, don't think so. There were really rather extensive changes under McNamara to humanize the apparatus, but I suspect that under Laird humane considerations may diminish or, at best, hold their ground.

Speaking of the spate of protesters, I knew something of one, a Cpt Noyd. He has been imprisoned for having refused to train pilots. In some respects, he is the most courageous and most unfortunate of them all. Not an inductee like CPT Levy, Noyd was a career Air Force officer. He had just finished his Ph d at Michigan in '63 under the man who became my advisor, A W Melton. Melton himself been either a field or general grade officer dealing with Air Force research at San Antonio, I believe, before retiring and accepting a professorship at Michigan. Being in the Air Force and having been a good student, Noyd was Melton's pride and joy. Noyd went forthwith to Colorado Springs, teaching Psychology at the Air Force Academy. Not a bad circumstance for a man who loved flying and experimentation. Enter the war and the Air Force's greater need for pilots than Psych profs. Noyd was reassigned and by all appearances would soon find himself flying combat missions. He sued the Air Force asking for either a guaranteed non—combat assignment or a release from the Air Force. ( Sometime around May 6 '67 it was on the front page of the NY Times) My recollection of exactly what transpired is rather foggy, but I infer that the Air Force later assigned him to training pilots. He apparently refused and was court martialed at a base near Hobbs New Mexico— about as far out of sight as is possible within the US. I believe he appealed once unsuccessfully and is now serving time. When I applied for readmission to Michigan, I asked Melton for a letter of recommendation and, half out of for what assumed to be his genuine concern and half out of curiosity, asked him what was the latest about Noyd and asked that he extend my sympathy. Melton had lost touch, Whether out of Melton's choice or Noyd's being too busy, don't know, but it was just another sad aspect to Noyd's rather considerable tragedy.

Rather than tire you further I'll draw this meandering message to close.
Yours affectionately
Walt

One set of students I knew from 1963 was Jim and Linda Lee Taylor. We travel with them in their camper Volkswagen mini- bus to DC for the purposes of protesting the war.


Jim and Linda sold us their house in Whitmore Lake. They also sold us some furnishings. They were traveling light; Jim had a post doc with The London School of Economics. 

z607 ✓

Grove, Box 192 Whitmore Lk, MI (~1970-06 — 1974-11)
1970-1979

We hadn’t anticipated owning a house so soon, but Jim and Linda Taylor needed to sell their home, and home ownership seemed like such a good idea that, why not?

I wasn’t happy about some of the features of semi rural living. 362 Grove required its own water supply, having a shallow well and veteran pump and pressure tank, it required its own septic service and a drain-field; it was surrounded by tall trees, many of which could fall on the house (one did); the house was initially constructed as a summertime, lakeside getaway, later retrofitted with an oil tank, furnace and some insulation for year-round occupation. The water was so mineral laden that the metal shower stall and the plastic curtain were permanently colored a rose-golden tan. Jim had made polyethylene storm windows for the enclosed front porch that one really needed for year-round occupancy of the front porch/office.

There were some unique pluses; Jim had added a photographic dark room to the basement’s water pump, hot water heater, furnace access space. And he would let go of his Czech made, Meopta Opemus II photo enlarger and other dark room equipment cheap. The one car garage, unlike the house, was relatively spacious. It could hold our car, motorcycle, and trailer when the trailer was tipped-up on its end.

Jim and Linda had refinished a round oak dining table and spindle back chairs. Pat and I have had the table and chairs ever since - including two crossings of the equator. There were other student level furnishings, most of which failed to make one of our seven crucial moves since 362 Grove.

Some friendships conveyed with the house: the elderly couple Jim and Louise Townsend, our only property line neighbor. Across the access street lived Jim and Pee-wee Gilliam who were generous in sharing the woodland knowledge Jim had picked up in West Virginia. Other neighbors were friendly to varying degrees but we had less attachment.


Finally, there was Boo the dog who conveyed too; I think Boo may have come with the house when Jim and Linda bought it. Boo was shaggy enough to endure Michigan winters; she had a house with abundant oak leaves banked up against it. While Boo was adequate to the winters we were ignorant of her vulnerability to heartworm which claimed Boo’s life in ‘72 when the Vet’s cure, as warned, prove fatal.

Neighborhood Child, Veronica , 1971-1972

Whitmore Lake, MI 48189 

Veronica, I soon became aware, was an extraordinary child. Ten, twelve years old, approaching the threshold of puberty, she was long and leggy for her age. Her cheer was persistent and inextinguishable. Open and accepting of people, the mere proximity of a neighbor made her blossom a cheerful greeting which lifted, if only temporarily, the negativity which clouded the everyday life of the lumpen proles about her. For it was a rather poor neighborhood. None appeared to be entirely free of the threat of the loss of employment. Changes in employment were common and the brightness in the eyes of their children was unbearably precious because it could not last for long.

Veronica was instantly a continuously shining moon in a sky that had there-to-fore known only twinkling stars. Her very name was a surprise. Not a Tammy or Linda or Barbara or other stylish, conventional name that betrays a conventionality of thought on the part of the parents. Nor is it the strained strident independence of a name like Guinnevere-Gan that merely notes another convention. How could such parents have come to buy that small cramped house in this neighborhood of small cramped houses and people? For the parents must be remarkable to have instilled so much trust in their eldest daughter. Failure of health? There must be some tragic liability which forced the parents to endanger their hopeful bright moon in this wetland wood where gloom prevails. My mind was always open for clues.

I did not actually seek contact with the Hepners, but mere proximity demanded that some contact occur. Danny was handsome, medium dark and mustached. For months his rust wracked work car supported a sign announcing his candidacy for union office. He offered to give me his remaining fuel oil when he had the furnace converted to gas. I discovered that Veronica's piano teacher was awaited the day that I brought over some potted fall flowering crocuses (which to my shame proved exceedingly reluctant to flower.) The once majestic maple in front of Danny's house was dead before Danny acquired the house. When Danny cut it down he helped me drag the massive trunk up the street to my yard where I use logs as a kind of fence.

.But not everything I saw made sense in terms of my preconceived notions about Veronica's parents. Veronica obviously received her physique from her mother, a not unpleasant, nervous woman whose quick smile was ingratiating, almost obsequious, rather than warm. A controlled tension predominated over the great potential for smooth gliding grace in her walk. Her post-adolescent brother lived in the house with them. The brother's presence provided me with the first glimpse of a ruffled feather among Danny's smooth easy feathers. At Veronica's mothers behest, Danny cut down the fruiting mulberry, the only tree that continuously shaded part of the house but which stained the paint on their good car. One night I overheard Danny volubly defending his brother-in-law against a stranger who had literally, in a rage followed the boy to his home. Propriety denied my curiosity more cues than that of the careless, endangering use of a car or was it the threatening of a car by the boy.

The brother-in-law had been in trouble before. Now, how did I acquire that cue? Yes,. . . it was from Danny, but why? Did he simply lose control or was he too open to feel the need for control of such information?

Winters foreclose on tenuous contacts among neighbors of convenience.

One day, during the first extended dry period in spring, my wife and I returned from work to be greeted by Veronica's mother who was extremely agitated and apologetic. The very vigor of her agitation almost made me lose control over the apprehension created by this unprecedented appearance in our yard. She had been burning spring leaves and the fire spread to my compost pile burning off the top. She could not accept my relative indifference, but continued to apologize profusely until she became aware of her repetition.

Across the street from me, in a house adjacent to the Hepners a marriage was shredded when the woman left with the husband's friend and the 3 children.

Partly because of the physical and temporal and structural proximity of the events I was utterly incredulous when I learned the Danny ran away with a nineteen year old girl.

Danny's house was vacant at odd hours, and light shone dimly through the windows at odd hours, and Danny's maroon and rust work car came to be replaced by a white Pinto which I think belongs to Veronica's maternal grandfather.

I met the grandfather once because in a grand drunk in the first blank hours of a Saturday morning he mistakenly pulled into my driveway, not once but twice. He was unable to get nearer to the house by his own devices. In controlled pity and irritation, I took his lighter from him and lit his cigarette. After a few fumbled tries, I found a key to a lock in his mind and he let me drive him and his car the rest of the way home.


And Veronica never smiles at me anymore.


I have even tried the, unprecedented for me, ploy of initiating a cheerful greeting, but so far I haven't attempted one that she could not ignore. I see her smile among the children with whom she plays, but I have not seen her whole face smile.

Veronica walks about more and further She has a new and frequent companion, a boy about her age, but a little bit shorter and his face has some of the hardness of a premature maturity.


With a baby on the way, we needed far more washing capacity. There was no room in the house, so the garage was the only option. The water lines had to be at least 4 feet below the surface, then emerge inside the garage. The washer and dryer had to be in an insulated, heated box to prevent freezing.

I was badly failing to address my Ph d dissertation requirement, troubled with asthma that presented itself when I tried running with Boo. (Even though I was hampered, the poor animal could not keep up with me; this was the first understanding that Boo was in trouble.). Like the 1964 dissatisfaction with Psychology, I was no more attracted to any branch of the discipline in 1972 than 8 years before. When I studied relevant journals, I found the text would roll beneath my eyes once, twice, four, even eight times with no retention. The perseverance I thought I gained in OCS was of no avail. I needed help, If nothing else, maybe the practical application of Clinical Psychology might be my answer. Maybe even a branch of Psychology I would find meaningful. I'd lay my problem at Clinical's doorstep.

I'd imagined an appointment with a member of the Clinical Psych's academic staff, keeping their hand in the trade. To my initial chagrin, Carol Barbour proved to be younger than me, looking like a younger student with her long raven hair straining for her tush. I strained but failed not to look disappointed. Almost obligingly on the next and all subsequent appointments she transformed herself, ordering her abundant hair into a matronly bun atop her head.

Pat and I attended Lamaze  birthing classes, I supported birth, and took pictures with pushed GAF dye based film.

The earliest meetings with Carol Barbour overlapped with the birth of Philip and my transition to a parent, a new set of responsibilities that did nothing to ameliorate my academic slough.



writes Walt, December 23, 1972 about travel via Chicago to Minneapolis, concerns for us, disenchantment with U S, current address.

Atlanta, GA 



Dear Walt & Patricia,

I do not know if you can ever forgive me for being so shitty about correspondence. I apologize for not writing before now. I have in my desk file under Bronstad about six unfinished letters to you I am writing now in the Atlanta airport, between planes to N. O where I will spend Xmas with my daughters.

First of all - I did reach Chicago in that dented, dusty Cadillac last August and from there bussed into Minneapolis. I enjoyed the time spent with you both very much. Then, as now, I wish we had more opportunities for direct communication. Since that time I have found satisfactory living quarters, am adjusting to the bitter, brutal weather and making new friends slowly, while clinging to a few special friends in in faraway places. The majority of my time and energy - much more than I anticipated contributing - has been involved with the exciting research I'm dong. I will tell you of my work later in more detail, but, to sum up this experience so far, the work has been demanding and rewarding, but I am very lonely personally. Thank you again for the fond remembrances of the outset of this venture.

Secondly you must both be proud parents now, experiencing the joys of diaper-changing and late-night awakenings . . . and the tremendous feelings of love that are communicated between a curious, groping, dependent infant and the two people who conjoined to bring him (her) into existence.

In my previous (unsent) letter fragments, I had put down many speculations about child-rearing as the thought of you as two parents caused me to focus on this question. However, now, I am not sure how many of those earlier thoughts may be dated or inappropriate since there has been such a long interruption in our communication So - I won't convey much along these lines now since so much would be speculation.

I was a bit concerned, and confused, when I left in August about Walt's seemingly excessive rationality, and Pate's undercurrent of hostility, That is, it seemed to me at the time (if I may be so presumptive and arrogant to speak thusly and not offend), that Pat might have been resentful of Walt's not having finished school by then, or even committed himself to some longer program. That Pat felt guilty about these harsh evaluations of Walt and tried to suppress them only to have them surface in the form of meddling or bickering about "non-issues" - all the while placing a lot of blame on herself for not being more tolerant, or stoical. Walt seemed compulsively rational, and perhaps tormented himself for not being able to resolve some basic questions about the future as well as being unable to suppress all emotion. I wonder if a child has served to catalyze a new understanding and cooperation between these two people I care about. Or whether the pressure of another force in the household has simply eclipsed for now the longer, still unsolved questions. We talked about these things, but there wasn't enough time, we were stoned, and your Danish friend [Ib Thompson] was leaving. Also Walt was starting psychotherapy [Carol Barbour] at the time.

It is 3:30 AM; that is my only excuse for not being more coherent and descriptive . But I have been thinking about some very basic observations that have been in my mind since visiting you. Perhaps not knowing how to deal with these uncertainties prevented me from writing before now.

Maybe some of my experiences can be beneficial. In reality(!) I am totally disenchanted with life in this country; my political analysis tells me that it is impossible to function here without serving the bourgeoisie (sp), capitalist ruling class; yet to renounce my citizenship is not a solution. The escape from this dilemma has been my work. That is my work serves as an effective evasion of more relevant, substantive questions I should be confronting myself with, but that is not the motivation for my working. The research is itself inherently challenging and stimulating enough. So, this fall I have been very busy, and overtly untroubled (also my own moral crisis of conscience has been put off by other problems relating to my children, finances, etc.). At any rate, I think we may share some of these frustrations (on top of our individual psychological hang-ups). Perhaps the right research problem can solve many things indirectly. It is still an escape, of course.

On rereading, this letter sounds horribly depressing and heavy. It occurs that I may be projecting a lot, and you twoare existing in a world of bliss. Prudence tells me to close before I get in too deep. I care about you both.

Love Tom.

Once Matt was mobile, we needed a way to keep him out of the street. I put up a T-post fence and a sand box which the cats thought was for them.

I'd commuted with the Yamaha R5 350 twin motorcycle ever since El Paso. To stop plug fouling I used plugs that were hotter; one summer that caught up with me. The hotter spark plug helped burn a hole in the piston. I tried to fix it myself, but I didn’t fix it correctly; a wrist pin circlip didn’t seat properly. It drifted out and badly scored the cylinder, effectively killing the engine.

writes Tom Mitchell , January 30, 1973 about Tom's travels, parenting, Philip

362 Grove
Whitmore Lake, MI 48105


Dear Tom,
I'm not retaliating for your being tardy about writing, I'm just bogged down in inertia and learning to endure it.

I trust you enjoyed your vacation in New Orleans with your daughters . Glad to see you find your work exciting. More likely, I'm envious, in moments of acute self-pity it is hard to recall work that was both demanding and rewarding, gratifying.

As you may infer I 'm trying to respond to your letter almost as if we were conversing; questionable form at best. Yes we are indeed now parents parents of a dominating horophagic baby boy. All is not familial bliss, there are to be sure times when I'm immensely gratified, proud and all the standard appropriate shit. But they ain't much sanction or even mention of the range of the negatives. Oedipus complex my ass, it was the father who was royally fucked over. I miss being mothered, being the center of the household, having a quiet household. When does it get better/ ?

Poor kid, Philip Matthew, we're already pushing society on him: watching his rate of growth, parading comparison figures of progress before other parents, frightened lest he be merely normal. Parental revenge already out— lined. The Calculus by age nine... Battle lines already drawn before he is weaned.

Yea, what are you observations on child rearing; I obviously need to dilute some ineluctable fantasies with a bit of reality.

What was your concern over my excessive rationality. At first blush that does not mean very much to me beyond the Norman Brown or Theod. Roszak perspectives. Ignorance of emotional factors, relevance? I confess ignorance, especially of my own feelings. That's about all learned in five months of tense unproductive therapy. Even yet I've got months of defenses, burnished bright by the therapist's patience, arrayed against her. If only she would have the common decency to cease being so attractive, perhaps I could unfreeze for more than a few scattered moments

Pat's hostility you mentioned. I'm not sure I can adequately recall the time to even have a hope of being on the right trail. I suspect that rather than your hypothesis of hostility derivative of my having failed to complete school Pat may well have been hostile because of my distance from her, my immersion in funk. Summer or rather the end of summer was an execrable time for me. Dirempted of hope from pushing a boring thesis topic, loss of friends, termination of an ambivalent affair and the concomitant guilt, the prospects of a not too welcome child: the stuff of funk. No Pat and I have hardly identified our problems, let alone approached an understanding ? acceptance? love?

Pat denies much anxiety about my indirection convincingly. Well. not so convincingly, I can't fully believe her denial because what she is denying sounds so plausible, and it is so comforting for me to accept the denial. But she did acknowledge hostility, and I'm not convinced that we've approached the basis. We probably should try marriage counseling or married group encounter.

Which brings me to the remarkable similarity of experience, of you and us and the Fox's. You remember Mike Fox Star-Telegram sorry if you'd rather not remember. Centrifugal thirties. Dope and pressure and thirties; my god, thirty and I'm not god yet.

Well maybe more dope. I remember your comment about worrying when you found yourself smoking more than once per week. Tried a week straight? What are your objections? Amotivation? Is dope the symptom rather than the cause? Physical damage? Back to Fox. He's mellowed and hardened. The first day with him was the best; he had finished writing a movie and worked thru the filming with the producer, (a Blaxploitation potboiler with a typical momophobic Fox undercurrent) Lightly stoned I followed with tolerant joy Mike in his unbounded ego trip. It was really tonic. For the first time I realized that Mike's flights are absolutely essential for him, without them he'd have to submit. I suspect that I've got to revive mine or go down.

Remember Jr High and High school book reports? Inevitably someone who had read a book for the first time ; Roger Sponge and his Paper Route or some other adolescent potboiler; invariably ended his book report with something like "and I think ever' one should read it." Well I've read another one an' I think 0 .. But really, I found Castaneda's Teachings of Don Juan and A Separate Reality fascinating. Castaneda is a grad student working on his Ph.d, studying ethnobotany. In his field rambling he encounters an old Mexican Indian reputed to know a lot of ethnic uses of botanicals. Mercenarily, Castaneda befriends the old man, who over an extended period decides that C will become his apprentice. Great! A wealth of data, except being an apprentice involves something like total immersion. Not just total immersion like total immersion in a language, but total immersion in a radically different culture, a radically different way of viewing reality, an ineluctable reality that alternately laughs at confounds, and trounces upon the reality that you and I know is, well real, isn't it? Carlos Castaneda's writing is so straightforward, so ingenuous, that when he clearly doesn't know which reality to believe, you are left wondering, "well maybe my reality is inadequate."

Whatever else one can believe, I find Mike Fox's almost offhand comment about Don Juan capable of sending chills up my spine: " Drug are going to be the Indian's revenge on the white man." Guns swept the world before the European. Now will the patient, disparate remains of the third world, whom we thought were groveling in the mud prove to be more powerful, prove to be consolidating their alliance with Nature. Are cannabis and paneolus, and peyote, and other 'weapons' that can grow in any mud unavoidably superior to guns by virtue of their vitality. The fields of susceptibility are apparent and growing. No longer just jazz musicians, No longer just the Beats. No longer just the Ghetto. No longer just the Campus. No longer just the High School, Jr High, Elementary, Parents, Grandparents.

Well to change the topic, and I'm really serious, can you whip up soma mescaline I get you the recipe? Psilocibin?

I'd like to have your reaction.

Take good care of yourself, damn it!
Love
Walton
writes Walt, April 25, 1973 about invented words, shared work at Star-Telegram, hallucinogens, and future travel.

Dear Walton,
I don't fault your logic but your rape of the language: "eating time" may be as reasonable as the colloquialism "eating pussy" for cunilingus, but that is hardly a defense of your invention, much less a basis upon which I am expected to deduce the meaning of your coinages. Let me rely on Latin and Greek roots and spare me chore of having to consider Americanizations, slang, and connotative alterations in my digestion of your new words (no logo phage me). Despite the fact that is gives me some pleasure (mainly because you're brighter than I am to tell you that you are wrong, that I am winning this logomachy, I nevertheless did understand perfectly what you meant when you first used the word horophagic in reference to Philip (I may be sorry I confessed that).

While bathing about a fortnight ago, my mind drifted off (aquatic psychomobility) to days of the Star-Telegram. I remember Bill Carter, Loren Brown, Tommy Stephenson, Mike Fox, and a few others whose names I can't dredge up from the recesses of my subconscious. I remember a big, stocky—fat guy with olive skin who drove a '51 Ford (actually, fifteen years ago, I drove a '51 Ford) who spoke lovingly, and not unpoetically, I might add, about the pleasure to be derived from fingering one's asshole. I remember a friend of yours and Fox's who used to complain that his cock was too large, actually becoming an impediment (as opposed to impetus) to his satisfactorily fucking and wasn't this true, he claimed to be compelled by this condition (penomegaly?) to be highly restrictive in his choice of female sex partners. Foreman Gore, who, in four years of Saturday nights, I never saw smile. An old—timer (one of the union men who stamped and mailed papers every night), who could construct a paper hat in seconds, came over to me was once-- I was reading Bacon during a press breakdown -- and began talking about his readings in philosophy, his unachieved goals and romantic dreams. Remember the press stoppages. The brown bags. Late night enchiladas and Monterrey specials at a greasy spoon down the block. Picking balls of black ink from our noses as we moved our smelly, tired, sweat— soaked bodies into the dewy air and drove back to our parents' houses, occasionally stopping for fried eggs or donuts and pinball. The transition between night and day. Balancing on guy wires and turning valves at the Shit Factory. I remember being teased a lot. I was terribly insecure then and with maintaining a masculine posture, which depended directly upon what I thought the other guys thought of me. I remember losing a bloody fight in the parking lot one morning after work. Despite losing, I was proud of my performance (or perhaps just not chickening out), felt more comfortable thereafter around the toughs at work, and less susceptible to bullying. The lessons I probably learned were that fighting was not very terrifying after all and much less of an emotional hassle than worrying about avoiding a fight. You, Walton, seemed more emotionally mature than me at that time, less dependent upon the opinions of others for your self—esteem. I really admired you then (and still do) Strange what things the mind chooses to remember.

Thank you for sending On Caring. It hag been very helpful. Too often I think, in our correspondence, we talk at, but not necessarily to each other. Period.

About three years ago a batch of good publications on chemistry of hallucinogens appeared. I have obviously ignored your repeated requests for dope on the synthesis of psilocybin; however, I am now convinced of your seriousness. I shall send you some references on this subject.

Tomorrow I leave for a week in N.O., then to Miami Beach for the convention of the Amer. Soc. for Microbiology. I still harbor hopes of getting together with you and Pat and Philip sometime this summer, Take care.

Tom
[ Thomas G. Mitchell, PhD
Associate Professor Emeritus
Department of Molecular Genetics and Microbiology
Duke University Medical Center]
One summer an earnest young bicycle evangelist stopped at our house. We talked at length about the virtues of bicycling and I became a convert. I bought a Sears 10 speed and tried, on the week end, cycling the 10 miles from 362 Grove to ISR. The first time was exhausting, but after a week or so it became fun to watch the red wing blackbirds command a marsh of cattails, a farmhouse wood pile expand from 10 yards long in spring to 25 yards long late autumn, chicory blossom and flower, farmers crops grow to harvest, frost announce the inevitability of winter and the end of cycling, for my lungs did not like air lower than the mid twenties.

The university would not let me dawdle endlessly, my assistantship ended after 4 years. That put me in a most awkward spot for the Arab oil embargo had knocked the American economy into a slump, most especially the automotive (Michigan) part. Jobs were getting harder to find. I started employment as a night watchmen for a security agency. NGK, a Japanese bearing company had a plant in Ann Arbor.

A semester later, my G.I. bill ran out. Since Pat was not working, the sole source of income was from whatever job I could find. I got a much closer watchman job at O&S Bearing in Whitmore Lake.

My Father was getting frail, Mother too. She worried about being able to continue caring for Clyde; that made me aware that I didn’t know how to take care of elderly either. But ignorance prompted me to take a job at the Whitmore Lake Convalescent home which was very much closer to home. It paid less, but I learned more.

1980-1989

Norris Dr Austin, TX (~1975-11 — 1985-06)

writes Family in Portland, OR, December 09, 1989 about Embrey's typewriter, visiting Hamilton home place, visiting Clyde in Clifton and feeding him ice cream, eating breakfast with Bette, Embey and Rexanne.

4710 E Loraine
Fort Worth, TX 76117


Dear Kate, Kris, Matt, Pat,

When I first tried Embrey's typewriter, I thought it was encoded. I typed "Dear Kate" and 'zSoa¯H01S" came out. Drat!, [I] thought this silly typewriter has the capacity to write In code, and I don't know how to turn the feature off. But thinking on it again, it occurred to me that the print wheel was mis-installed. Sure enough, with the print wheel in right, it comes out readable. The spelling correction ribbon ran—out so that doesn't work, but I hope you'll overlook my many typos.

Today's path was very familiar; old but familiar. You would recognize much of it. Out highway 183 past Cedar Park, Liberty Hill, Lampassas, to Hamilton. I climbed over the fence and walked past the stock tank that Mother and Father had built. You may remember "camping" overnight there once with Granny. It was gratifying to me to see that the pecan tree Mother had me plant there appears to be prospering. The home place was more of a shambles than ever before. Mother and her sisters used to resent, I think, that Uncle Embrey got the home place. But it meant little in real terms. He certainly had a sentimental attachment to it but it was really a liability, not an asset. He could put money into it (and I suspect he had a new roof put over it, but he ever realized much from it. It was mostly unoccupied after Grandmother Walton left. [There was an occupancy by grandmother Ethel's sister Jessie and her husband Sterling.]

I went inside a short distance, a little afraid of rattle snakes even though it was cold. What really halted me was a combination of apprehension that the floor could give way beneath me, that I would be started by any animal that fled my approach, and a feeling of pain to recall locations where my parents grandparents, aunts, uncles, cousins, etc. were still alive in my memory. Walking on to the Southwest corner of the property I saw the tank Uncle Embrey had made. It had water too, Further up the hill jack rabbits startled me twice. The grass looked relatively healthy: it was not overgrazed. The troublesome part of the fence on South line, the part adjacent to the southwest corner looked ready to fall over. The tiny house on Red Bird hill looked no worse than before. The windmill however was running uncontrollably. Very bad. The wire that stops it from running was broken, so its been running uncontrollably for who knows how 1ong. Eventuality it will run itself to destruction. I need to be there some calm day, when the wind isn't turning the mill, so I can deactivate it. But I need someone with me in case I fall off.

I got ice cream for Father. That is about the only effective interaction I have with him; feeding him much as one would an infant. It's a most curious mixture of infantile behavior (unable to feed himself, unable to converse. unable to control his bodily functions and his identity as Father, the epitome of an adult role. My adult model, in every way one recognizes person. He is that person, yet he most obviously is not. Our categories just don't hold. Adult—child these are distinct aren't they? No, in him adult and infant are one. Such is the way of life and death. Yet I still try conversation I ask him questions, tell him about visiting the home place and sometimes he responds appropriately with a "yes" or "sure good" about the ice cream or "Do you know Mom and Dad" (his Mother and Father. Or "My family's all left me") So I. don't know what to make of it. Is his experience of the world like a foggy dream. Is his mind relatively aware of the present but his tongue difficult to command, much like one's arm that has "gone to sleep", that feels funny or doesn't feel at all and doesn't work right when you try to use it. If we judge solely by his speech, we may cheat the awareness that he does have, cheat it of the knowledge he would like to have.

Bette and Embrey are fine. The three of us and Rex Ann ate breakfast out. I pitched the ball to Embrey then she to me. I still hit the ball better than she. She to wants to play chess now.


Love Walt

"Hello Kate, write Embrey soon - Embrey.

Letter's Context

2026-08-30


My old neighbors, George Kegley and Nancy Lynch, loaned me their 1719 Norris street home when I first relocated from Portland Oregon to Austin in November 1989. I believe Paulette Gravois may have engineered this arrangement. As if that weren't enough generosity, they loaned me George's car to drive to visit my Father in Clifton Sunset Home and Sister at 4710 E Loraine. Pat, staying back in Portland until school semester's end, had the 79 Honda, all I had was my bicycle for transport.

Mother had died August 18 of that year; many stresses of her life, professional and familial took final toll. Clyde had been for many months at Sunset Home, I had last seen him there in the winter of 1987-88 when my family visited the US from New Zealand. Clyde had been much more coherent then, emotive, talkative and connected. Well at home in his surroundings; he knew some of the residence of Sunset Home from childhood.

The path mentioned was familiar because my family had taken it many times, visiting from Austin to Fort Worth where many of both my kin and Pat's kin lived. We took that path, longer than the I-35 path, because it was scenic and because it connected with my childhood, albeit not Pat's.

Father joined Mother in death on the 26th of the same month, December.

1990-1999

DeVerne, Austin, TX (~1990-01 — 1990-06)

writes Mike Fox, March 27, 1993 about Linda's funeral, her familial, psychological and medical problems, Ina Jo's burdens, Mike's photos and Branch Davidians.

2501 Side Cove
Austin, TX 78704-4533
(512) 441 4998


Dear Mike,
 

Well, the photos came out after all. I felt sure they had been failures. The camera has been acting incorrectly for about a year. Too damn expensive to get camera work done. About a month ago I tried cleaning the electrical contacts between the camera body and the lens. That seemed to resolve the intermittent incorrect exposure problem, but because of the problem, I've pretty well gotten out of the habit of taking pictures.

I still take it along. To funerals and all. Hell, if people didn't die, I'd have no social life at all. This time another cousin. It was in Odessa instead of Fort Worth or points north, otherwise I'd have stopped by. Should have really, the family would have enjoyed my absence if longer. The cousin, Linda, was relatively close. Close in childhood and adolescence, as close as people get to me, which isn't really very close. Years, miles, diverging values, the labors of every day life, and her psychosis separated us.

In the last 30 years, I've seen her once. At her brother's funeral. Shocking. Seeing her that is; I know I've aged. Face, belly and butt keep growing, sucking in the hair. Ditto her; but more. She'd been in a collision. Like a car the insurance company totaled, and an industrious Lebanese family restored to casual presentability, nothing fit quite right. Her eyebrows had been plucked within an inch of their life; her head was permanently bowed; she spoke upward, if at all, as a supplicant. She smoked like a car needing rings, and her speech was at half speed, if that fast. The content was gratitude that I parked by her., the problems of her medication, and the conflicts with her Mother. My Mother had told me of Linda's extensive shock therapy. I know Linda had left her husband and two children. It appears, that about a week ago, Linda left Linda, by choice. Maybe by accident. The family hopes that. Confusion under mind altering medication may lead to confusion over dose timing which may lead to over medication and a fatal cycle. My own hope is for a rational decision for an end. The truth is probably in between. Daughter Mother conflict played a role.

Aunt Ina Jo asked me to stay the night with her. Her losses have been heavy. Her husband, Uncle Ernest was my adolescent model of manhood. Self made, industrious, courtly, a man of good cheer, earnestness, and attentive kindness for all regardless of age, he set a standard I've abandoned aspiring to. Its too hard. His addiction to chewing tobacco, one suspects, had a role in is death to throat cancer in '81. Their son, Dick, my greatest friend of childhood and early adolescence, rode the family business down to an inevitable crash in an atmosphere of low oil prices and changing technology. He died in 90 of asthma.

So when this old woman now bereft of immediate family, one of my Mother's two surviving siblings, asked me to stay the night, I was both honored and filled with pity. She asked me to take pictures of Linda's grave. That was the end of the roll of film, and guess what popped out with Linda's graveside flowers? Your Chinese scroll. Hope it's not too late for your purposes.

Enough of the light stuff. A serious scientific hypothesis. About halfway between us 100 or so people await Armageddon, or some bad shit whichever comes first.

Amid the swill of incredible revelations is the picture of some of the faithful, working their fingers to the bone on the farm, working a job in Waco, turning over their paychecks and then Turning over their wives to Vernon (reborn as David Koresh) Howell. Geese! Gullible, wet-behind-the-ears, dewy mouth babe credulous, trusting fish. And it's fully 7 years before the next millennium.

As the calendar creeps closer to 2001 will we be prepared to gauge the crescendo of honking geese who cry alarm over the calendar's upcoming revolution? At the last millennium, barely numerate Europeans were a-clatter with the certainty that the new millennium meant Jesus was due again. There is a continuous millennealist hum in the body politic. Every now and then a little group will go off to some chosen corner to await rapture. The time when their faith and sacrifice will reap the payoff of a big win to heaven. Just as the Texas lottery seems especially popular in central Texas, so is that other long odds bet. Betting it all on the second coming.

One way of watching this anticipated parade is to compare the participants. Sure numbers will be interesting. Just like reporters like to announce the gate at baseball games, so we will want to know the "gate" at the rapture runoffs. But there are other social -psychological variables that deserve attention. My main interest is in gullibility. But science advances by quantifying variables. We don't accept hot and cold any more for most purposes. We measure the temperature.

The Celsius scale is bounded by the boiling point of pure water at normal pressure at sea level (100 degrees Celsius) and the freezing point of pure water (0 degrees).

I propose that the unit of gullibility be the howell (after Vernon Howell). The high end of the scale is 1000 howells. It takes that much gullibility to hand over your paycheck and your wife to Vernon. The bottom end of the scale, 0 howells is an act such as: You cash a check for $18.73 at first national bank, and you count your money again before leaving the counter of your Mother the career teller. That's no gullibility.

Calibrating Scale of Gullibility
Howels For Acts of Gullibility
1000Turned over paycheck and spouse to Vernon Howell
900Entrusted one's child to Father Powell (Massachusetts, New Mexico, Minnesota)
800Entrusted one's child to New Mexican Archbishop Robert Sanchez
700Gave virginity (any sex) to any priest in New Mexico
600Believed Vernon Howell would do what he said.
500Bought into Jim and Tammy's vacation resort
400Voted for Bush's "No new taxes"
300Buy lottery ticket regularly
200Buy Sears vinyl siding without competitive bid
100Felt relief when funds released for [Reagan's] "Star Wars"
0Re-count cash less than $20 received from Mom the career teller

Sincerely,

Walt Bronstad


writes Bob Holbrook, April 9, 1993 about Claibourn's funeral, Bette, Dink, Embrey, Beulah, Winston, Leone, Jill, Helen, Shelby, Herman, Horace, Robbie, Florence Ann, Glen

2501 Side Cove
Austin, TX 78704-4533
(512) 441 4998


Dear Bob

Claibourne Walton's funeral was Wednes­day, April 7, 10 AM at the Lometa Church of Christ. It had rained heavily most of the way from Austin to Lampassas; Bette told me it had rained heavily on her as she drove from Ft Worth to Hamilton. I came alone; Bette brought Dink and Embrey. Beulah couldn't find anyone to take her place, so she didn't come.

The church was nearly full; the immediate family came in last. Leone led, the way, in a wheel chair pushed by her son Winston. She got up slowly from her chair and set in the pew. I knew Helen and Shelby, Robbie and Florence Ann. Glen and his wife were there too. Many were from Leone's side; and I didn't know them. Herman Walton and Glen Walton were the only pall bearers I knew.

During the service songs were a cappella. The minister said Claibourne was born Feb. 2, 1907, I believe. He died 4/5/93. The pro­cession to the grave site seemed endless. I would have trouble retracing the path now, except by remembering we went through Star. Sorry, I forgot the name of the ceme­tery, maybe you know it; it's where Aunt Ina and Dr. Hicks are buried, I be­lieve. Maybe the Mores too.

We drove back by highway 84, to Evant. Then through Adamsville on to Claiborne's house where we ate a lunch.

I met Leone's brother Doris, Winston's wife, Jill, and their children Clay and Molly. (both children are working on advanced degrees at Texas Tech). Cynthia told me she lives and works in Houston. Winston teaches in Lam­passas. Glen has a home for emotionally disturbed children in Lometa.

Dink wasn't very well; she said her doctor told her that her blood count was O.K. so she could stop chemotherapy. She said her mouth still wasn't well (inferred that chemo­therapy interferes most with rapidly grow­ing cells; that would include tissues lining the mouth and digestive system.) Dink could walk all right, but she didn't like to stand for long. At the grave site she left for Bette's car to sit and rest while Bette con­tinued visiting.

Herman Walton was there; (with his wife, I presume, I did not speak with her); Herman and I spoke briefly. He looked in good health; there was vigor in his manner, but his pace was cautious. Could have been the abundance of mud.

Leone seemed bright and alert. but she looks so very frail. Helen looked well too, so did Shelby. I wasn't aware of it, but a comment Helen made alluded to Shelby's having been through a course of chemo­therapy.

I wrote the above chiefly for Ina Jo and Annie. Had they been in better capacity, I'm sure they would have been there. Clai­bourne, as you know was a double cousin; biologically, that looks as close as a sibling. Of course it's not quite the same as a sibling. Living together in the same family is its own power that in cases like Linda transcends biology. Or does it?

You may be in a better position than I to understand the perspective Linda might have known. In the family, but not wholly of it. In the wake of Linda's tragic life and tragic death (and I must admit in flagrant disregard of unquestioning loyalty to Aunt Ina Jo) I have wondered if Ina Jo applied unfamilial pressures on Linda to conform to some un­reasonably idealized familial norm. Linda, knowing she was adopted, had limited re­sources for responding to those pressures.

I'm not sure where all that comes from. Partly from idle curiosity. Partly from identi­fying with Linda. Partly from a feeling that Ina Jo may well have all her oars in the water, but her oarlocks will be among the last to need grease.

And that dubious judgment probably has its origins in some of the even more dubious psychological theories that attached all man­ner of neuroses and psychoses to defective parenting.

Back to terra firma. You asked at some point what more a word processor could do than could Microsoft's Write. This letter has a few examples. Some are obvious: arrang­ing the text in columns, the capacity to "draw a large initial letter to tell the reader where to begin. Others are less obvious, but nonetheless useful. Spell checking, hyphena­tion, font switching, are some of many tools one can use to make a better looking letter.

Please let me hear from you.

Love

Walton Bronstad

writes to Bob Holbrook, April 10, 1993 about spring leafing out, Pat's running, Matt, Kate and Kris in school.

2805 W Capitol Dr
Appleton WI 54915

2501 Side Cove
Austin, TX 78704-4533
(512) 441 4998


Dear Bob and Charlotte,
 
How are the pictures coming? Better than My check, I hope. I hadn't forgotten, just my ususal procrastination.
 
It's been tiresomely muggy here for half a week. Finally, it's begun to rain a little. A rather dry spring thus far.
 
The Oaks are fully leaved, the maples waited a little, but they're coming along. Pat's ginko is about half way to full leaf. Bluebonnets, buttercups, blue stars are at their prime. Our photenia flowered well for the first time. Pat hates their perfume, finds it cloying.
 
Pat entered the Capitol 10,000, a 6 mile run. She intended to walk the distance, but wound up running most of the way. (Today, she's so sore, I drove her to work. Really its here knee that troubles her.) Out of 17,000 runners, she didn't see a soul she knew. Obvious, isn't it, all her contemporaries have more sense.
 
Kate seems to have done rather well on her PSAT test. A number of schools have sent her literature. She turned up her nose at Bryn Mahr. She still has the brochure for Oberlin. I glance et the $25,000 annual cost and got woozy.
 
Kris wants to attend an AIDS conference in New York this summer. I don't get to find out much of what's going on with her. This weekend she was a student panelist at a planned parenthood meeting. Don't jump to any quick conclusions. I think its more a reflection of her kind of social concern rather than sexual activity.
 
Which brings us to Matt who has been spending more time away from home than at home. If he comes home at all, it'll 'be around dinner time.' Since most of that time away is either at work, or school or with his friend Charlaine, we don't worry about him as much as years earlier.
 
Charlaine seems to have her feet firmly on the ground. If nothing else develops from their relationship, it should help him mature.
 
Tell us how things are going with you. Tell us about Terry, Rex, Mark and families.
 
Love
 
Walton Bronstad

wrote to Bob Holbrook, May 3, 1993 about family in Fort Worth, working in side Cliff yard, MS Word, Pat's job, and Matt, Kate and Kris.

2501 Side Cove
Austin, TX 78704-4533
(512) 441 4998


Dear Bob and Charlotte,
 
Summer is here; its past midnight and I'm sweating. I don't want any more summers in Texas, but short of the ground, I don't know how to pull it off.
 
Pat, Kate and Kris are in Ft Worth. Pat let the girls drive, a little in Haltom City. This she tells me after the fact, probably to save me the added anxiety. Kate and Kris spent some time with Embrey, Pat a little time with Bette. Pat also ate lunch with all but two of her sisters. She'll return tomorrow.
 
I've spent a little time with the yard, and painted a lit­tle more of the fence, cleaned the pool a little and spent more time with my toy. I've been working through a book on design using Word for Windows, but a little frustrated because all the examples required a diverse set of fonts. Found some today, at half price, so now I can play with different typefaces.
 
For most purposes, standard ones that come with Word are quite satisfactory, even much to be pre­ferred. Familiar typefaces are designed to be more readable; they're the one's to use for extended text. More dramatic ones are harder to read, and should be used sparingly, chiefly to set the tone.
 
That's probably more than you ever wanted to know about typography. It's slowly dawning on me that one can't go everywhere the computer can go.
 
How is your work with the computer? Full of big frustrations and little gratifications, one imagines, if your experience is anything like mine.
 

!

Pat likes her job. Some days the stress is more than she would like. She proudly displayed her first publication, an exam schedule. It's widely read, that's more than many authors can say. We expect it will have a very short life though. The reading public is fickle.
 
Matt is fine. He'll be even better when he decides what career he wants. So far nothing really grabs him, nothing that pays, that is.
 
Kate is fine too. She is the most directed of the three. More accurately, the one more conven­tionally directed. She works hardest in school, gets the best grades. She won second place in her division in the science fair. She has been getting straight A's (except she got a B this last 6 week period. She cleans the pool, cooks (sweets mostly). See what I mean about "conventional." It's refreshing.
 
Kris is trying to push the back end out of uncon­ventional. Her room may get us run out of the neighborhood. For a while she spent much time with her
 
computer, writing.

!

The check is for the flowers which I was supposed to pay half for and never have. Better late than not at all. Tell me if it's short, and by how much.
 
Love
 
Walton Bronstad
wrote to Travis Bronstad, Aug. 30, 1993, about Bette not going to Germany, Bob might work in Tennessee,and auto insuring daughters.
923 Denton
Denton, TX 76201

2501 Side Cove
Austin, TX 78704-4533
(512) 441 4998


Dear Aunt Travis,

 
That last two times I talked with Bette she told me that she was seriously thinking about a job in Ger­many. The next time I had talked with her she had declined. There were just too many minuses this time.
 
Since the American dollar is worth about half its value the last time she went, since she would have to move to another loca­tion several months af­ter she got there, and since she would have a hard time finding someone reliable to look after the Loraine house, she decided, re­luctantly not to go.
 
I tried to tell her that Embrey Sophia would have a rough time too. German schools are considerably ahead of ours, Embrey's German was probably just ade­quate for the work and social contact of ele­mentary school years when they left. Em­brey's language skills would not have kept up with the vocabulary expected in gymna­sium. She would have the double burden of demanding classes and the handicap of poor language skills. More­over she would have to change from American high school, to German gymnasium, then to another gymnasium in months. Too much, far too much.
 
Bob called Sunday; what a surprise; he's talking seriously of taking a job in Nash­ville, Tennessee.
 
I worry for him; that's quite a radical change. Charlotte seemed to accept the idea rather well. It seems that work at his current lo­cation has seriously dried up, and the near term prospects don't look good. Thus the need to move on.
 
He noted that it's not so very far from where Papa Walton grew up in Kentucky. Just where, exactly, in Ken­tucky did the Waltons come from?
 
Regarding Homer and Travis. Bette tells me Homer is having more trouble swallowing, but Bette has not visited lately; nor have I.
 
Here in Austin, every­one is in school but me. Matt is a Junior at UT. Kate and Kris are Jun­iors at Austin High. Pat is working at UT so we can pay for 3 teenagers. This sum­mer Kate and Kris took driver's ed. Kate got her driver's license. Passed the test the first time, but it wasn't as easy as she thought it would be.
 
What a shock for me though. After Kate got her license, I called my insurance company. How much to cover Kate?
 
“Does she have her li­cense?”
 
Yes.
 
“What is her license number?”
 
Its 175....
 
“Well she's already covered and you annual premiums are $1200 higher.”
 

!LfhbL


 
Kate declined my bribe to surrender her li­cense. I said I'd rather pay Kate the $1200 for clothes, than pay the insurance company.
 
“No thank you, I want to keep my license.”, replied Kate sweetly, knowing full well I've got to buy her clothes anyway.
 

!LS\fhbSL


 
Not to worry, Kris heard of the bribe. Typically, not having applied herself as as­siduously as Kate, Kris was somewhat less likely to pass the test so readily, but she let me know she wanted the bribe.
 

!LhbYNS\i]fhL


 
So today Kris, whose sense of style brings Charlie Chaplain to mind is chauffeured to school by Kate.
 
I swear, I never saw it coming; I'm just too old to play this game.
 
Let me hear from you.
 
Love
 
Wally
